So:
- Position 269: V (Valine)
- Position 270: H (Histidine)
- Position 277: Y (Tyrosine)
- Position 281: Q (Glutamine)

Now, the question asks which residue has the &quot;proton donor/acceptor role.&quot;

Histidine is the classic proton donor/acceptor in enzyme active sites. Its imidazole side chain has a pKa of approximately 6.0-7.0, making it ideally suited to act as both a proton donor and proton acceptor at physiological pH. This is why histidine is commonly found in the catalytic triads and other catalytic centers of enzymes (e.g., serine proteases with the His-Asp-Ser triad, or in various other enzyme mechanisms).

Valine (V) is a hydrophobic amino acid with no ionizable side chain - it cannot act as a proton donor/acceptor.
Tyrosine (Y) has a phenolic hydroxyl with a pKa around 10, so while it can be a proton donor, it&#x27;s not typically described as a &quot;proton donor/acceptor&quot; in the same way as histidine.
Glutamine (Q) has an amide side chain that is neither a proton donor nor acceptor in the traditional sense.

Therefore, position 270 (Histidine) is the answer, which corresponds to option C.

This is consistent with what we know about class D β-lactamases (OXA-type), where a conserved histidine residue (often His67 or His120 in different numbering schemes) acts as a proton shuttle/proton donor-acceptor in the catalytic mechanism.

The answer is C (Position 270).

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this kinase, which amino-acid residue is assigned the proton-accepting role in the active site?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Glutamate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Aspartate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysine<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>**Step 1: Identify the protein as a kinase.**<br>The sequence contains hallmark kinase motifs:<br>- A **Gly-rich loop** region near the C-terminus: &quot;GIGDTISSSAFVSE&quot;<br>- A **DFG-like motif** (GD here): &quot;VGIGDT&quot;<br>- A **catalytic loop** resembling HRD: &quot;IDAMNVLMDETG&quot;<br><br>**Step 2: Identify the catalytic residues in the kinase active site.**<br><br>In the canonical protein kinase mechanism, three key catalytic residues are present:<br>1. **VAIK-Lysine** — binds ATP α-phosphate and helps orient the γ-phosphate<br>2. **DFG-Aspartate** — coordinates Mg²⁺ ions<br>3. **HRD-Aspartate** — acts as the **catalytic base (proton acceptor)**<br><br>**Step 3: Determine the proton-accepting role.**<br><br>The proton-accepting role refers to the residue that accepts a proton from the substrate&#x27;s hydroxyl group (Ser/Thr/Tyr -OH) during phosphotransfer. This is the **catalytic aspartate** located in the HRD motif of the catalytic loop. In this sequence, the region &quot;IDAMNVLMDE&quot; contains this catalytic aspartate.<br><br>In well-studied kinases like PKA, the HRD-Asp (Asp166) is universally assigned the proton-accepting/catalytic base role. This is a conserved feature across protein kinases.<br><br>**Step 4: Eliminate other options.**<br>- Lysine (D): Binds ATP phosphate, not a proton acceptor<br>- Glutamate (B): Typically part of the αC-helix salt bridge, not the proton acceptor<br>- Histidine (A): Not the catalytic base in canonical kinase mechanism<br><br>The proton-accepting role is assigned to **Aspartate**.<br><br>2. answer: C</td>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> For this kinase, which amino-acid type is assigned the active-site proton-accepting role? Distinguish a residue’s general acid–base plausibility from the protein-specific assignment.<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Histidine<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Glutamate<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Aspartate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Lysine<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> active_site_0000:2<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>To determine which amino acid type is assigned the active-site proton-accepting role in this kinase, I need to identify the catalytic machinery.<br><br>**Sequence Analysis:**<br>This protein contains recognizable kinase motifs:<br>- **VAIK motif**: &quot;GKEKVFEII&quot; — contains the catalytic lysine that coordinates ATP phosphates<br>- **DFG motif**: &quot;STVGIGDT&quot; — the DFG aspartate coordinates Mg²⁺<br>- **Catalytic loop (HRD motif)**: I can identify &quot;RD&quot; within &quot;LKEYYPDGTTYRD&quot; in the catalytic loop region. The aspartate (D) in the HRD motif is the key catalytic residue.<br><br>**Mechanistic Analysis:**<br>In protein kinase catalysis, the proton-accepting (general base) role is assigned to the **aspartate residue of the HRD catalytic loop**. This aspartate abstracts a proton from the substrate hydroxyl group (Ser/Thr/Tyr -OH) during phosphoryl transfer from ATP.<br><br>**Distinguishing general plausibility from specific assignment:**<br>- Histidine (A): Has excellent general acid-base chemistry (pKa ~6.0, near physiological pH), making it a plausible proton acceptor in *general* enzyme chemistry. However, in the specific context of kinase active sites, the histidine of the HRD motif plays a structural/positioning role, NOT the proton-accepting role.<br>- Lysine (D): Has a high pKa (~10.5), making it a poor proton *acceptor*; it acts as a general acid (proton donor) or for electrostatic stabilization of ATP.<br>- Glutamate (B): Not typically part of the canonical catalytic machinery of kinases.<br>- **Aspartate (C)**: In the HRD motif, the aspartate is specifically assigned the proton-accepting (general base) role in the kinase catalytic mechanism.<br><br>The question specifically asks about the protein-specific assignment in this kinase, not general acid-base plausibility. In this kinase, it is the aspartate of the catalytic loop that accepts the proton from the substrate.<br><br>2. answer: C</td>
